The verse widens the address: ***“And ask those who were given the Scripture”*** and ***“and the illiterate ˹people˺, “Have you submitted yourselves ˹to Allah˺?””***. The two groups are the Jews and Christians on one side and the Arabs who had no book on the other, as Ibn ʿAbbās identifies them, and al-Saʿdī notes that the question puts both on the same footing. Neither lineage nor learning protects a person from the question, since the verse asks the same thing of the people with a scripture and the people without one. What follows is that the community being built in Madīnah is measured by the same standard as everyone it meets.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the passage in view with the verses before it, where God's oneness was witnessed and the religion accepted was named. Since the question is about submission rather than about the finer points of law, the answer is available to every hearer. Al-Baghawī notes the plain sense of the asking, and the sentence is short enough for a child to repeat. That is part of its strength: the question cannot be escaped by saying the discussion was too difficult.

**THE OUTCOME OF THE TWO ANSWERS**

The verse then sets out both outcomes. ***“If they submit, they will be ˹rightly˺ guided.”*** and ***“But if they turn away”*** and ***“then your duty is only to deliver ˹the message˺.”***. Al-Ṭabarī draws the limit of the messenger's responsibility from this, since the turning away of a hearer does not fall on the one who delivered the message. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the same line and notes that the verse advises the believer against endless disputation with people who have already decided. The duty is to carry the message clearly. The outcome is not in the carrier's hands.

The sentence closes with ***“And Allah is All-Seeing of ˹His˺ servants”***. The ending is tied to the two answers just described by al-Qurṭubī, since the One who sees His servants knows who submitted and who turned away, and the difference between the two is not lost in the crowd. Think of a teacher who marks the papers himself rather than trusting a summary. No student's work is confused with another's. The verse ends with that sight, and it leaves the reader with the only question that remains after all the arguments, which is which of the two answers his own life has given.

(4:166 — **“Allah bears witness to what He has sent down to you”**) stands in the same line as the testimony named earlier in this passage, and it places the delivery of the message in the hands of the One who sent it. The duty of the messenger and the duty of the community are one and the same in that respect: deliver clearly, and leave the account to the One who sees.

---

## Verse 3:21

> Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly, and kill people who stand up for justice—give them good news of a painful punishment

**A CHARGE THAT CLIMBS FROM DENIAL TO BLOOD**

The verse gathers three offences and puts them in one sentence: ***“Indeed, those who deny Allah’s signs, kill the prophets unjustly”*** and ***“and kill people who stand up”*** and ***“for justice give them good news of a painful punishment”***. The order is not casual, since denial comes first and murder follows it. Al-Ṭabarī works through the wording of the third offence, and the older readings differ over whether the killers are described as fighting the prophets or as putting them to death. The difference does not change the charge, which is that blood was shed over a message.

Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the three offences together, and he explains the last of them as the murder of the people who urged the oneness of God. Al-Jalālayn records the number of prophets said to have been killed, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān carries the account al-Ālūsī brings from an earlier report of the scholars, in which a company of prophets was put to death at one time and the pious men who stood up to demand justice were killed the same day. The verse follows one crime with another because the pattern repeated itself: an attack on a prophet was followed by an attack on anyone who defended what the prophet had brought.

**THE ANNOUNCEMENT THAT IS NOT GOOD NEWS**

The command then says ***“give them good news of a painful punishment”***, and the words are a deliberate turn. Al-Jalālayn notes that the announcement is meant as a jeer, since the words of good news are used for the opposite of what they normally carry. A man who is told to expect a welcome and is given a sentence instead knows at once that the language has been turned against him. The verse does that on purpose, and the effect is heavier than a plain threat.

Al-Qurṭubī keeps the report of the offenders for whom the passage was first given, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the shift in the sūrah at this point, since the earlier verses answered the Christians and these words address the people of the earlier Scripture who had acted against the messengers. What follows is a lesson about how a community can keep a scripture and still produce murderers. The book on the shelf does not decide what the hand does, and the difference between the two is named for the reader.

**WHAT THE VERSE SAYS TO A LATER READER**

The order within the verse teaches something that reaches beyond its first audience. The first offence is disbelief, and the second is violence, and the third is the murder of the defenders of justice. That sequence is the way such things actually travel, since a man does not begin with blood. He begins by deciding that a message need not be heard, and the decision grows into a hand. Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as a rebuke to the people who had received a Scripture and then acted against its messengers, and the rebuke is a warning to anyone who inherits a book without inheriting its obedience.

Think of a man who finds a crack in the wall of his house and paints over it every year. The paint hides the line, and the wall keeps moving. The verse refuses the paint and follows the crack to its source, and the reason is that a community which does not name the first step will never stop the last. Al-Ṭabarī's treatment of the sentence keeps each of the three offences distinct, so that no reader can collapse them into a single ancient crime. The verse is a description of a road, and the road is drawn so that the traveller can see where he is on it.

What the verse ends with is a sentence of judgement, and the judgement is placed in the hands of the One who sends it. The command to announce the punishment is given to the messenger, and the punishment itself is left with God. That division is the whole of the answer to a violent age: state the offence, name the outcome, and leave the account where it belongs. The verse is short, and it sets up the sentence that follows about wasted deeds.

---

## Verse 3:22

> They are the ones whose deeds are wasted in this world and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers

**THE DEEDS THAT COME TO NOTHING**

The verse draws the consequence of the crimes just named, and it does so in a single sentence: ***“They are the ones”*** and ***“whose deeds are wasted in this world”*** and ***“and the Hereafter. And they will have no helpers”***. Al-Jalālayn explains the wasting as the failure of whatever good a man may have done in charity and kindness to kin, since the work was built on a foundation that has collapsed. The sentence does not say that nothing was done. What remains is nothing.

Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same sense and adds the second half of the loss, which is that the reward is withheld in the world to come while the benefit in this world is gone as well. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the two worlds are named together because the loss covers both of them, and the man who loses both has lost everything he had to lose. A person may spend his days building a house, and if the ground gives way, the years of work are not counted as a house but as a heap of stones.

**WHY GOOD WORK CAN BE WASTED**

The verse teaches something about the way God measures a deed, and it does so without any long argument. The good a person does is accepted when it stands on the foundation the messengers brought, and the people named here rejected that foundation and killed those who defended it. What follows is that effort alone is not the measure. A river carries water in proportion to the height of its source, and the same rain falls on the mountain and the plain without making the plain a river. A deed has a height as well as a volume.

Ibn Kathīr reads the promise of the previous verse as the reason for this one, since the announcement of punishment is matched here by the account of what the punished will carry. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the sequence in the sūrah, with the earlier reproof of the Christians followed by the account of the people who went further in their opposition. Since the same Book is in their hands, the loss named here is not a loss of information. It is a loss of everything built on the information they refused to obey.

**NO HELPERS ON THAT DAY**

The sentence closes with ***“And they will have no helpers”***. Al-Saʿdī explains the phrase as the removal of every support a person might hope for, and the reports of the chapter have already spoken about wealth and children that cannot save anyone. A man may have friends who would move a mountain for him, and the day of account is the one day their hands cannot reach. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the plain sense, and the ending is total because it leaves nothing standing beside the person.

Think of a man who fences a field and never digs to see the ground beneath it. The fence looks the same on the last day as on the first, and the field is gone. The verse digs under the fence for the reader, and what it finds is the reason a person cannot rely on the width of his work. That is why the sentence is placed next to the account of three crimes. The crimes are the surface, and the wasted deeds are the foundation that was never repaired.

---

## Verse 3:23

> Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scriptures? Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly

**THE QUESTION THAT OPENS A SCENE**

The sūrah turns from the ruin to the conduct that produced it: ***“Have you not seen those”*** and ***“who were given a portion of the Scriptures?”*** and ***“Yet when they are invited to the Book of Allah to settle their disputes, some of them turn away heedlessly”***. The question is addressed to the messenger and through him to the reader, and it invites a look at a scene that had already taken place. Al-Ṭabarī reads the invitation as the call to let the Book decide, and the turning away as the refusal of a decision that had not yet been given.

Al-Jalālayn records the case behind the scene. Two of the community had committed a serious offence, and the judgment they sought from the messenger was the stoning the Torah carried. The account is kept by Ibn ʿAbbās with the tribes named, and al-Qurṭubī gives it with the place where the discussion happened. The one who turned away was not turning away from a strange ruling, and that is the sharp point of the verse. The book they honoured was brought and consulted, and the verdict in it was the same.

**THE INVITATION AND THE REFUSAL**

The invitation in the sentence is to the Book of God, and more is carried by the call than a procedure. A person who accepts an arbitrator accepts the possibility that he will lose, and the people in the scene had already decided which outcome they would accept. Al-Qurṭubī's account shows the sequence: the judgment was given, the scripture was produced, the same judgment was found inside it, and the two offenders were punished while a party among them went away angry. The anger came after the proof, which is why the verse can describe the turning as heedless rather than as uncertain.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the passage censures a particular claim of the people of the earlier Scripture, and that the claim sits in the next verse where it belongs: they had told themselves that the fire would touch them only for a few days. That belief is what made the refusal possible. A man who thinks the account will be short will risk the account, and a community with a discount waiting at the end of its sins has already paid the first instalment of them.

**WHAT THE SCENE SHOWS THE READER**

The verse is a picture of a group meeting a book it already possesses. Nothing in the scene requires a new revelation, since the record they carried contained the ruling. Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as part of the rebuke to the people of the Book, and the specific failure he names is that they turned from a judgment they themselves had asked for. The verse does not accuse them of ignorance. It accuses them of preference.

Think of a merchant who asks for an audit and then objects to the auditor when the ledger is opened. The objection is not about the numbers; it is about the man's own position. That is the picture the verse paints, and the phrase that closes it is exact: they turn away and are heedless at the same time, since the two things often travel together. What follows from the scene is a question for every reader who holds a book, which is whether the book is being used to find the judgment or to avoid it. The verse leaves the reader with that question and with the account of the day in the next verses, where the answer is settled.

---

## Verse 3:24

> This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying

**THE CLAIM THE VERSE NAMES**

The turning in the previous scene is now given its reason: ***“This is because they say, “The Fire will not touch us except”*** and ***“for a few days.” They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”***. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the claim in the form in which it was made, and al-Jalālayn records what the few days were counted to be: the span during which their forefathers had worshipped the calf, after which the punishment would end. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the number and the calculation that supported it, with each day measured against a thousand years. The claim was not a carelessness. It was a comfortable system of arithmetic.

Ibn Kathīr reads the sentence as the explanation of the refusal that came before it, since a man who expects a short sentence and a light one will not submit to a ruling he dislikes. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same order and notes the claims the community had built for itself, including the claim to be the children of God and His beloved. What follows is a picture of a religion kept for its privileges rather than for its Lord, and that is the picture given in a single line.

**THE DECEPTION AND WHERE IT CAME FROM**

The sentence closes with ***“They have been deceived in their faith by their wishful lying”***. The deception is named as their own invention, and that is the sharpest word in the verse. Al-Jalālayn treats the lying as the thing that did the deceiving, so that the community is both the author and the victim of its own claim. A man cannot sue a mirror for what he has painted on it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān calls the position a self-deception, and the phrase is exact, since the false comfort was manufactured at home and then trusted as though it had come from a prophet.

Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the sense of the invented claim, and al-Qurṭubī's discussion of the passage notes how a community reaches that point. The reasoning runs in a circle: a privilege is assumed, the account is shortened to fit the privilege, and then the shortened account is used as proof of the privilege. The verse breaks the circle by naming its first link, which is the invention itself.

**THE WARNING THAT REACHES FURTHER**

The verse is aimed at a specific claim, and the shape of it is universal. Any person can build a private assurance about how his account will end, and the assurance can be dressed in religious language. Since the claim here was about a few days in the fire, its modern relatives are the claims that a person's group, lineage, or past deeds guarantee the outcome before the account is opened. All of them are named in one word: invention.

Think of a traveller who is warned about a broken bridge and answers that his family built the road, so the bridge must hold for him. The road is real, the family is real, and the bridge falls the same way for every car. That is what the sentence about deception describes, and the next verse shows the road arriving at the broken place. The claim of a short punishment is answered, in the verse that follows, with a gathering that no arithmetic can shorten.

---

## Verse 3:25

> But how ˹horrible˺ will it be when We gather them together on the Day about which there is no doubt—when every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged

**THE DAY THE ARITHMETIC MEETS ITS ACCOUNT**

The claim about a few days is answered with a question: ***“But how ˹horrible˺ will it be”*** and ***“when We gather them together on the Day about”*** and ***“which there is no doubt”***. Al-Ṭabarī draws the sense of the question out, since the verse is asking what their condition will be on the day they meet the account. The answer is left for the reader to complete, which is a heavier treatment than a description. A question about a state makes the hearer picture himself inside it.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the address here changes to include the messenger and his community, since the state is worth considering for everyone who hears it. The day is named as one about which there is no doubt, and the certainty is placed inside the sentence so that the reader cannot treat the gathering as a topic for discussion. Al-Jalālayn keeps the day as the day of resurrection, and Ibn ʿAbbās fills it with the raising of every soul after death, righteous and sinner alike.

**WHAT EVERY SOUL RECEIVES**

The measure of the day follows: ***“when every soul will be paid in full”*** and ***“for what it has done”*** and ***“and none will be wronged”***. The payment is in full, which means nothing is rounded away in either direction, and the closing phrase guards both sides of the scale. Ibn ʿAbbās explains that no good deed will be diminished and no evil deed increased, so the two halves of the sentence protect the just and warn the unjust at once. The same reading is kept by al-Saʿdī, and the fairness named here is the reason the earlier verse's arithmetic collapses.

The phrase also answers a quiet hope that runs in every community: that a favourable connection will adjust the account. The soul is paid for what it did, and that is said in the middle of a passage about a group that trusted in its standing. Nothing in the sentence mentions lineage, book, or name, which is the point. A scale that can be leaned on is not a scale, and the sentence describes an exact payment without exception.

**THE QUESTION A READER IS LEFT WITH**

The verse closes the passage about the people of the Book, and it does so by removing the distance between them and the reader. The passage is joined to the account of the claim about the fire by Ibn Kathīr, since the two belong together as claim and answer. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān keeps the sequence in view and notes how the sūrah returns to the theme of the account after each digression. The Book works like a physician who returns to the wound after treating the symptoms.

Think of a merchant who has kept two sets of books and is told that the auditor is coming on a fixed day. The day does not negotiate, and the second set of books does not survive the reading. That is the picture the verse leaves with the reader, and it is placed immediately before a passage about power and honour being given and taken by God. The connection is deliberate: on the day when the account is exact, the only honour that counts is the one granted by the One who keeps the scale.

---

## Verse 3:26

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O Allah! Lord over all authorities! You give authority to whoever You please and remove it from who You please; You honour whoever You please and disgrace who You please—all good is in Your Hands. Surely You ˹alone˺ are Most Capable of everything

**THE PRAYER THAT ANSWERS A LAUGH**

The sūrah puts a prayer in the messenger's mouth, and the prayer begins ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O Allah!”*** and continues ***“Lord over all authorities!”***. Al-Jalālayn records the occasion: the community had been told that the lands of Persia and Byzantium would be opened, and the hypocrites called the claim absurd, and this verse was sent down. The answer to a laugh is not an argument but a declaration about who holds the keys. Since the promise had been treated as impossible, the One for whom nothing is impossible is named.

Ibn ʿAbbās explains the address as a request to be led to good, and he reads the title that follows as the acknowledgement that God owns every kingdom and every king. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes that the passage teaches the believers a prayer that carries an announcement inside it, since the words ask for nothing that the promise had not already granted. A community told to pray this way is being trained to expect the opening of the lands as a gift rather than as a conquest. The prayer and the promise are the same message seen from two sides.

**THE FOUR CLAUSES THAT SWING ONE WAY**

The middle of the prayer moves in pairs: ***“You give authority to whoever You please”*** and ***“and remove it from”*** ***“who You please”***, then ***“You honour whoever You please”*** and ***“and disgrace who You please”***. Ibn ʿAbbās names the first pair with the community of Muḥammad on one side and the empires on the other, and he gives the second pair the same shape, with the Prophet ﷺ honoured and the leaders who mocked him brought low. Al-Ṭabarī treats the four clauses as the account of a single power, since the hand that gives is the hand that takes away.

The order within each pair is worth noticing. The giving comes before the removing, which means the sentence begins with mercy and ends with the warning that the mercy is not owed. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading, and the effect is that a king cannot read the verse as a promise to himself. He holds what he holds because it was given, and the same sentence that put it in his hand can take it out. Think of a steward who is handed the keys of a house and told that the owner may return at any hour. The keys are real, and the ownership is not his.

**THE GOOD AND THE POWER**

The prayer closes with ***“all good is in Your Hands. Surely You ˹alone˺ are Most Capable of everything”***. Ibn ʿAbbās lists what the hand holds: honour, abasement, sovereignty, spoils, victory and strength. The sentence names the good as coming from God while it leaves the harm outside the naming, and al-Qurṭubī takes up the point that the harm is not attributed to God as a gift. What follows is a way of looking at loss that keeps a believer steady. A kingdom taken away was never a property of the man who held it, and a kingdom given was never earned by the one who received it.

The last words answer the laugh once more. Power that can do all things does not need a large army to keep a promise, and the verse fixes the reader's eyes on that power before the next verse lists the daily evidence of it in the turning of the night and the day. Since the creation runs on that power every hour, the promise about Persia and Byzantium is small beside the machinery already in motion. The prayer teaches the community to measure the promise by the power rather than by the enemy's numbers.

---

## Verse 3:27

> You cause the night to pass into the day and the day into the night. You bring forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And You provide for whoever You will without limit.”

**THE EVIDENCE OF THE HOURS**

The prayer continues with what every reader sees: ***“You cause the night to pass into the day”*** and ***“and the day into the night. You bring forth the living from the dead”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the wording as one thing entering another, since the hours of the night are absorbed into the day and the hours of the day into the night. The older explanation is kept by al-Qurṭubī, that the decrease of one is added to the other, so that the seasons themselves become a measurement of the same power that gives kingdoms and takes them.

The sentence is read by Ibn ʿAbbās as the lengthening and shortening of the two, and the observation is available to any farmer or traveller. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the same point from the sky, where the sun and the moon serve the days and nights at the command of their Maker. A court may not notice the change of the seasons, and the seasons do not wait for the court. What follows is that the power described in the previous verse is not a rare intervention. It is the ordinary running of the world.

**LIFE FROM DEATH AND DEATH FROM LIFE**

The middle of the verse turns to the beginnings of living things: ***“and the dead from the living.”***. Al-Jalālayn gives the examples plainly, since a human being comes from a drop and a bird from an egg, and the drop and the egg come from what was alive. Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same pair in view, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān widens the list to a tree from a seed and a seed from a tree. The verse is not describing a wonder that happened once. It is describing the routine out of which every reader was made.

The same sentence is used elsewhere in the Book, in a passage that joins it to the reviving of the earth after its death: (30:19 — **“He brings forth the living from the dead”**). The repetition matters for the argument of this chapter, since the promise of a resurrection is treated in the Book as a larger version of a process the reader watches every spring. A man who accepts the field's return after the winter has already accepted the pattern. What turns on the verse is whether he will draw the conclusion about himself.

**PROVISION WITHOUT LIMIT**

The prayer ends with ***“And You provide for whoever You will without limit.””***. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the sense of a giving that is not measured out against a store, and Ibn Kathīr notes the same in his account of the verse. The phrase is placed after the night and the day and after the living and the dead, so the order climbs from the clock to the cradle to the table. A reader who has followed the three steps has been shown the power, the making, and the feeding in one sentence.

Think of a household where the bread arrives every morning and no one ever sees the granary. The children grow up knowing the bread is real and never wondering where it comes from, and the granary is named for them. Since the provision is unlimited, the promise about the kingdoms returns to the reader in a new light: the One who feeds every bird can open a road to an empire, and the One who opens the road can also close the hand. The verse leaves the reader with the ordinary table and the ordinary sunrise, and it asks him to read both as letters from the same writer.

---

## Verse 3:28

> Believers should not take disbelievers as guardians instead of the believers—and whoever does so will have nothing to hope for from Allah—unless it is a precaution against their tyranny. And Allah warns you about Himself. And to Allah is the final return

**THE PROHIBITION AND ITS DISTINCTION**

The sūrah turns from the promise to a boundary: ***“Believers should not take disbelievers”*** and ***“as guardians instead of the believers”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the prohibition as a ban on making the disbelievers the people a believer leans on and trusts with his affairs. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the same line as a ban on the kind of familiar dealing that turns into loyalty, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the phrase about a trusted inner circle that the Book uses elsewhere. The point is not who a person greets in the market. The point is whose hand he holds.

**THE COST OF THE WRONG ALLEGIANCE**

The sentence then states the cost: ***“and whoever does so will have nothing to hope”*** and ***“for from Allah”***. The words are stark, and Ibn ʿAbbās reads them as the cutting of the bond between the man and his Lord. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading and notes that the loss is not a punishment postponed to the end. A person who chooses another hand to hold has already loosened his own, and the sentence describes the state rather than announcing a sentence. The verse does not say he will be fined. It says he will have nothing to hope for where hope matters most.

**THE EXCEPTION AND ITS LIMIT**

The exception is then given in the same sentence: ***“unless it is a precaution against their tyranny”***. Ibn Kathīr explains the permission as the case of a believer who fears harm from those in power over him and guards himself with words while his heart stays where it was. Al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the line carefully: a bond of love in the heart is forbidden, while ordinary dealings and the caution of a man in danger are permitted. A believer may lower his voice without moving his loyalty, and the verse knows the difference.

**THE WARNING AND THE RETURN**

Then the warning tightens: ***“And Allah warns you about Himself.”***. The phrase is repeated in the verses that follow, and al-Qurṭubī treats the repetition as the force of the warning. God does not warn a person about an enemy or a rival. He warns him about the One whose right it is to be trusted first, and that is the heaviest form the warning can take. Think of a father who warns his child about the child's own father, and the shape of such a warning is the shape of these words.

The verse closes with ***“And to Allah is the final return”***. Ibn Kathīr notes that the ending answers the question left by the prohibition, since the One to whom everyone returns is the One whose friendship cannot be replaced. Every tie a person makes in this life is a tie that will be tested on the road back to its Lord. (4:144 — **“Do not take disbelievers as allies instead of the believers”**) states the same rule in another sūrah, and (5:51 — **“Take neither Jews nor Christians as guardians”**) applies it to the communities of the Book. The rule is not a local policy of one city. It is a boundary drawn around the allegiance of the heart.

What follows from the passage is a question the reader can put to himself: when his affairs become difficult, whose door does he knock on first. The verse does not forbid kindness to a neighbour or fair dealing with a partner, since the Book commands both. It forbids the transfer of trust, because the heart cannot be carried on two shoulders at once. And the warning about Himself is placed just before the account of the day when every soul is shown its record, which means the two announcements belong to the same hour.

---

## Verse 3:29

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Whether you conceal what is in your hearts or reveal it, it is known to Allah. For He knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. And Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

**THE SECRET THAT IS ALREADY KNOWN**

The warning of the previous verse is followed by an announcement that closes every hiding place: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Whether you conceal what is in your hearts”*** and ***“or reveal it, it is known to Allah.”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the sentence as the statement that nothing in the heart escapes the knowledge of God, and Ibn ʿAbbās names the first audience, since the verse was given about the hypocrites and the Jews who carried a hidden enmity. The prohibition in the earlier verse was about loyalty, and the announcement here explains why the prohibition cannot be dodged by a quiet heart.

Al-Jalālayn notes that the knowledge named in the sentence covers the hidden loyalty itself, and that the power named at the end of the verse includes the punishment of the people who held it. The two halves work as a pair: the first tells the reader that the secret is seen, and the second tells him that the One who sees can act on what He sees. What follows is that a hidden alliance is not a safe middle position. It is a decision already on the record.

**THE KNOWLEDGE THAT COVERS TWO WORLDS**

The verse widens the frame in its next words: ***“For He knows whatever is in the heavens”*** and ***“and whatever is on the earth.”***. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the plain sense, since the One who knows the hearts of people also knows everything in the two worlds they live in, and the order of the sentence climbs from the small to the vast. A reader who has accepted that God knows the heavens will not struggle to believe that God knows his own heart. The verse begins with the heart, and the heavens are named as the enlargement of the same knowledge.

Ibn Kathīr reads the passage as a comfort as well as a warning, since the same knowledge that discovers a hidden betrayal also carries every good thought and every private intention. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the practical consequence in the dealings the sūrah has been describing: a believer who has to live among people whose loyalties are uncertain can rest his case with the One who sees what the world cannot. Since the record is kept by the One who made the hearts, no honest intention is lost at the door.

**THE POWER THAT CLOSES THE SENTENCE**

The verse ends with ***“And Allah is Most Capable of everything.”***. The phrase has already appeared in the previous promise about authority, and here it answers a different question. A criminal who believes he is unobserved is only waiting for the day when the observation becomes evidence, and the closing words state that the evidence can be brought at any time. Think of a man who hides a stolen coin inside the wall of his own house and tells himself that the wall is the one place no one will look.

The sentence also reaches a softer reader, since a person carrying grief or good intentions in silence may feel that his inner life is unseen. The opposite is said in the plainest terms, and the same power named at the end of it keeps the heavens running. What follows from the whole passage is a life lived in the open before God even where the world cannot see, and (2:284 — **“Whether you reveal what is in your hearts”**) has already taught the community the same lesson. The verse does not ask a person to broadcast his secrets. It tells him he never needed to, because the One who matters has already heard them.

---

## Verse 3:30

> ˹Watch for˺ the Day when every soul will be presented with whatever good it has done. And it will wish that its misdeeds were far off. And Allah warns you about Himself. And Allah is Ever Gracious to ˹His˺ servants

**THE DAY THE LEDGER IS OPENED**

The passage closes with the day itself: ***“˹Watch for˺ the Day”*** and then ***“when every soul will be presented with whatever good it has done.”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the presentation as the bringing forward of what a person did, so that the work stands in front of its owner rather than following behind him. Ibn ʿAbbās records the older picture of a written register, and the sense is the same in both readings: nothing is remembered by accident and nothing has to be searched for. The good comes out of the record and takes its place in the open.

The word that opens the sentence asks the reader to expect the day, and the expectation is the whole purpose of the account. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the sentence is tied to the warning that came before it, since a warning is only useful to a person who believes that the day will come. A man who reads the verse and treats the gathering as a distant event has gained nothing from the reading. The verse is a notice posted where the traveller passes it every day.

**THE WISH THAT CANNOT BE GRANTED**

The sentence then names what the soul will feel: ***“And it will wish”*** and ***“that its misdeeds were far off.”***. The wish is given its full shape by al-Jalālayn, since the soul will long for a distance between itself and its evil so wide that the evil can never reach it. Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading, and the picture is a person looking at his own record and hoping for geography to save him. The wish comes too late, and the verse places it in the text so that the reader can feel the lateness while he still has time.

Ibn ʿAbbās notes that the distance wished for is a distance of time, with the man hoping that the day of his evil had never arrived. The two readings meet in the same helplessness, which is that a deed cannot be separated from its doer after it is done. A man may burn a letter, but he cannot burn the writing out of his own hand. That is what the soul discovers on the day the register is opened, and the verse teaches the lesson in advance because the lesson cannot be learned late.

**THE WARNING AND THE GRACE**

The verse closes with the warning of the previous passage and a name that balances it: ***“And Allah warns you about Himself.”*** and ***“And Allah is Ever Gracious to ˹His˺ servants”***. The two sentences are kept together by al-Qurṭubī, and he treats the pairing as the whole of the divine address: the warning keeps a person from carelessness, and the grace keeps him from despair. Ibn Kathīr reads the closing name as God's kindness toward His servants, and the reports of the chapter have already shown what that kindness looks like in the form of guidance, forgiveness and provision.

Think of a road with a cliff on one side and a wall on the other. The warning is the cliff, and the grace is the wall. A traveller who has only the warning will not walk at all, and a traveller who has only the wall will not watch his step. The verse gives the community both in one breath, and (39:70 — **“Every soul will be paid in full for its deeds”**) states the same settlement in another sūrah. What follows from the passage is a life lived between two facts: the account is exact, and the Lord of the account is gracious. The warning and the kindness come from the same hand, and the verse ends with the reader invited to act on both.

---

## Verse 3:31

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If you ˹sincerely˺ love Allah, then follow me; Allah will love you and forgive your sins. For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

**THE CLAIM OF LOVE AND ITS TEST**

The verses that defined allegiance are followed by a verse that defines love, and it opens with a command: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “If you ˹sincerely˺ love Allah”*** and ***“then follow me”***. The occasion is kept by al-Jalālayn: a people said they worshipped what they worshipped out of love for God, hoping it would bring them near to Him, and the answer came in these words. Ibn ʿAbbās records the same claim among the people who counted themselves the beloved children of God. In both reports the claim is the same, and the verse tests it with a single movement: the love must walk.

Ibn Kathīr draws the judgement out plainly, since the passage settles the case of anyone who claims to love God and refuses the way of His messenger. A claim of love that is never translated into following is not a smaller love; it is a different thing wearing the same name. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān presses the point from the nature of love itself, since love is a hidden thing and needs a proof that others can see. That proof is following, and the proof is named before any reward is.

**WHAT FOLLOWING EARNS**

The sentence continues with the result of the following: ***“Allah will love you”*** and ***“and forgive your sins.”***. The order is startling, since a servant loves his Lord and then finds that the Lord loves him back, and al-Qurṭubī treats the exchange as the heart of the verse. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the promise as an increase of love, and al-Jalālayn keeps the same sense with the forgiveness of the sins committed before the following began. What follows is that the road of obedience is not a tax paid to a distant king. It is the way a servant walks into the love of his Lord.

The forgiveness is joined to the love in one sentence, and the pairing teaches something about how a person should read his own failures. A believer who loves God and stumbles is not thrown out of the road, since the sentence that promised the love also promised the wiping of the sin. Two things are joined here that people often separate: the standard is high, and the door is wide. (2:165 — **“they love them as they should love Allah”**) describes the love that gives a creature the place of the Creator, and the verse here describes the love that gives the Creator His place in a life.

**THE TWO NAMES AT THE CLOSE**

The verse ends with ***“For Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.””***. The two names match the two promises that came before them, since the forgiveness answers the sins and the mercy answers the love. Al-Ṭabarī keeps the older readings of the sentence and the sense is one in all of them. Think of a teacher who tells his students that he will walk with them himself and that their past failures will not be held against them. The offer is not a lowering of the standard. It is the standard carried by a hand that is willing to lift.

Ibn ʿAbbās records the reaction of one of the listeners, and the report is a picture of what the verse does to a claim that has been resting on lineage. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notes the sequence in the sūrah, since the oneness of God has been established and the following of the messenger is now added to it, so that the two together make the religion. The verse leaves the reader with a question he can answer in private, which is what his own claim of love has cost him in the way he walks. The answer is the subject of the verse that follows, where obedience is named in plain words.

---

## Verse 3:32

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Obey Allah and His Messenger.” If they still turn away, then truly Allah does not like the disbelievers

**OBEDIENCE NAMED WITHOUT ORNAMENT**

The claim of love is followed by an instruction with two objects: ***“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Obey Allah”*** and ***“and His Messenger.” If they still turn away”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the sentence as the plain command to obey God in what He has made obligatory and the messenger in what he brought, and Ibn ʿAbbās keeps the same reading. The pairing is the point, since obedience to God is not separated from the following of the one He sent. A servant who says he obeys the master and ignores the messenger has made a distinction that the sentence does not allow.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out the place of the verse in the sūrah, since the oneness of God was affirmed in the passage before it and the belief in prophethood is affirmed here. The two together form the entry into the religion, and the rejection of either is named as the same refusal. Al-Jalālayn notes the older grammatical point about the sentence that follows, and the meaning is unaffected by it. What follows from the pairing is that a community cannot honour God while sidelining the man He sent to teach it.

**THE TURNING AWAY AND WHAT IT MEANS**

The sentence continues with the alternative: ***“then truly Allah does not like the disbelievers”***. The people who turn away are not named as the disobedient or the forgetful, and that naming is deliberate. Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the statement that the refusal of the command places a person among those God does not love, and Ibn ʿAbbās identifies the first audience as the Jews and the hypocrites. Since the previous verse had promised love to the followers, the refusal leaves a person outside that promise, and the verse says so without softening.

Al-Qurṭubī keeps the passage short and points to the fuller treatment of obedience in another sūrah, and the brevity here is part of the force. A command has been given by God and carried by His messenger, and the only question left is what a person does with it. (4:80 — **“Whoever obeys the Messenger has truly obeyed Allah”**) states the same identity of the two obediences, and the verse here gives it in the form of a summons. The two passages are read together because they answer the same question from both ends.

**THE LOVE THAT IS WITHDRAWN**

The verse ends with a sentence about God's love rather than about a punishment, and that is the heaviest word in it. A person may bear a penalty and keep his standing, and the loss described here is the loss of standing itself. Al-Jalālayn reads the not-loving as the refusal of reward and the promise of punishment, and the two readings belong together. Think of a father who says of a son that he does not approve of what the son has become; the sentence is not a beating, and the son would rather have the beating.

The verse also guards the community from a false reading of the previous promise. The love promised to the followers of the messenger is not a possession that can be inherited by a claim, and it is not a title that can be carried without the walk. Since the sentence in the earlier verse was joined to following, it is joined here to obedience, and the two words describe the same road from two sides. What follows is a plain question for each reader: whether the message that reached him is being obeyed or being managed, and the answer to that question is the same as the answer to whether he is inside the love the verse describes.

---

## Verse 3:33

> Indeed, Allah chose Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham, and the family of ’Imrân above all people ˹of their time˺

**THE HOUSEHOLDS THAT WERE CHOSEN**

The sūrah now turns to the family whose story gives the chapter its name: ***“Indeed, Allah chose Adam, Noah, the family of Abraham”*** and ***“and the family of ’Imrân above all people ˹of their time˺”***. Al-Ṭabarī explains the choosing as the selection of these houses above the people of their age, and al-Jalālayn records the sense given to it, which is that prophethood was placed among them and their descendants. The verse does not say that the members of these families were all prophets. The line through which prophecy travelled was carried by them.

Ibn Kathīr works through the four names and shows what the choice looked like in each case. Adam was created by the hand of God, given life by His breath, taught the names of all things, and honoured by the prostration of the angels. Nūḥ was the first messenger sent to a people that had turned to idols. The house of Ibrāhīm received the covenant and the line of prophets, and the house of ʿImrān produced Maryam and the child she carried. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same material together, and the pattern that rises from it is that the choosing was always for a work rather than for a privilege.

**WHY THE NAMES ARE GIVEN IN THIS ORDER**

The order of the four names follows the shape of the story the sūrah is about to tell. Adam stands at the beginning of humanity, Nūḥ at the beginning of the mission to a straying people, Ibrāhīm at the root of the line that carried the covenant, and ʿImrān at the household whose daughter was the mother of ʿĪsā. Al-Saʿdī notes that the choosing of these houses is part of the answer to the delegation, since the honour of Maryam and her son is traced back to a chosen line rather than to a claim of divinity.

That is what turns on the sequence for the argument of the chapter. The visitors from Najran had argued from the honour of ʿĪsā, and the verse grants the honour and then places it in its family tree. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the older discussion of the names, and the point that comes through the reading is that the choice of a house is a gift of God rather than a rank that the house confers on God. A family can be lifted by its Lord, and the lifting does not change who the Lord is.

**THE MEANING OF BEING PREFERRED**

The phrase ***“above all people ˹of their time˺”*** keeps the preference inside a time, and the wording protects the reader from a misunderstanding. The verse does not say that every member of these houses was better than every other person in history. Al-Jalālayn reads the preference as the placing of prophethood among them and their offspring, which is a preference given to a line for a task. A road is chosen to carry a carriage, and the choosing of the road is not a claim about the stones.

Think of a village that has produced the teachers of a whole region for four generations. The honour belongs to the village, and the sons who teach still have to learn their lessons and rise early to teach them. The verse grants the honour to the houses and leaves the work inside them, and the story that follows shows the work being done by a woman who dedicated her child and by a man who prayed in a chamber for years. Since the preference belongs to a time and a task, it cannot be inherited as a rank. What follows is that a family's standing before God rises and falls with its obedience, and that is the point the verse is making about the chosen houses. That is the kind of preference the sentence describes, and the next verse explains why the houses are named together.

---

## Verse 3:34

> They are descendants of one another. And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing

**ONE LINE, MANY HOUSES**

The verse explains the list before it: ***“They are descendants of one another.”*** and then ***“And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing”***. Al-Ṭabarī reads the sentence as the statement that the chosen houses are one stock, since the line of the prophets runs back through them to a single origin. The children of Ibrāhīm, the house of ʿImrān and the line of Adam are not separate honours granted to unrelated families. They are one chain, and the chain is named so that no reader treats one of its links as a stranger to the others.

Ibn Kathīr keeps the same reading and adds that the sentence accounts for both the choosing and the family of the sūrah's name. Maryam's line is joined to the line of the prophets, and the verse makes that joining in four words. Al-Qurṭubī draws the point out for the argument with the People of the Book, since the community that honoured Ibrāhīm and the family that produced ʿĪsā come from the same tree. To honour one branch and despise the other is to fight against the tree.

**THE NAMES THAT CLOSE THE SENTENCE**

The verse closes with ***“And Allah is All-Hearing, All-Knowing”***. The two names are placed here because the choice described in the previous verse was made by the One who hears what every house says and knows what every heart holds. Ibn Kathīr notes that the knowledge covers the worthiness of the chosen, since a choice made in true knowledge is not an arbitrary favour. Al-Saʿdī keeps the same sense, and the effect is that the reader is not invited to audit the decision. The One who chose knows, and the sentence says so.

Al-Jalālayn keeps the sentence short, and the two names answer two kinds of doubt that a reader may bring. A person may ask whether God knew what these houses would do, and the second name answers him. He may ask whether God heard the prayers that rose from them, and the first name answers him. The verse does not discuss the doubts. The two attributes that dissolve them are the point, and the reader is left with a settled view of the household the sūrah is about to describe.

**WHAT THE SENTENCE MEANS FOR A READER**

The sentence is about lineage, and it ends about knowledge, and the two together teach something that reaches beyond the story. A line that was chosen still depended on the One who hears and knows, which means no house and no community can turn its history into a claim on God. Al-Qurṭubī's discussion of the passage keeps the community of the Book in view, and the warning is the same one the earlier verses gave: a book, a family, or a record of past service is not a substitute for obedience.

Think of a workshop that has passed its trade from father to son for a hundred years. The skill is real, the name is honoured, and the buyer still checks the work in front of him. The verse honours the line and closes with the two attributes that keep the honour in its place, and the story that follows shows a household at prayer rather than a dynasty at table. The next verses open the door of that household and let the reader hear what was said in it.

---

## Verse 3:35

> ˹Remember˺ when the wife of ’Imrân said, “My Lord! I dedicate what is in my womb entirely to Your service, so accept it from me. You ˹alone˺ are truly the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

**THE HOUSEHOLD THAT PRAYED**

The story of the chosen family begins in private: ***“˹Remember˺ when the wife of ’Imrān said, “My Lord!”***. The verse asks the reader to stand at that moment, and the first word of the prayer places the whole event in the hands of God. Ibn Kathīr notes that the woman is the mother of Maryam, and al-Qurṭubī keeps the reports that place her among the devout of her people. The scene has no crowd in it, and the honour of the house is traced to a prayer said in a room.

The prayer continues with a vow: ***“I dedicate what is in my womb entirely to Your service”***. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the dedication as a pledge that the child would be given to the service of the sanctuary, free for the worship of God, and al-Jalālayn keeps that sense. The wording covers the child before anyone knew whether it would be a boy or a girl, which means the mother gave what she did not yet have. A promise made before the gift has arrived is the purest form of one, since nothing is being paid out of a pocket that is already full.

**THE HOPE INSIDE THE REQUEST**

The sentence then says ***“so accept it from me. You ˹alone˺ are truly the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.””***. The request for acceptance is followed by the two names that make the request possible, and the pairing is exact. A Lord who hears the words of a private prayer and knows the intention behind them is able to accept a gift that no one else has witnessed. Al-Qurṭubī keeps the same reading, and the sense is that the woman did not present her vow to a distant authority. She presented it to the One who had already heard it.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws the lesson for parents from the passage, since the first recorded act of this household is a decision about what the child would be for. The mother did not begin by planning a marriage or a trade. She began by handing the child to God, and the story that follows shows how that decision was answered in a way she had not imagined. Al-Saʿdī keeps the same line, since the acceptance she asked for was granted in a form that carried more than she had pledged.

**WHAT THE VERSE LEAVES WITH THE READER**

The verse closes the opening of the family story, and it leaves the reader in a room with a woman praying. Everything that follows in the sūrah depends on that prayer: the birth of Maryam, her service in the sanctuary, the provision that came to her, and the child who would be given to her later. The honour of the house whose name the sūrah carries began with a promise made in secret, and the Book preserves the words so that the promise is remembered.

Think of a man who plants a tree in his courtyard for a child not yet born. He will not eat from it, and he may not live to see it bear, and the planting is still the most practical thing he does. The verse shows the mother of Maryam doing exactly that with a life, and the story that follows shows the tree. What turns on the passage is the way the Book traces greatness back to its source, which is not an army or a palace but a whispered promise made to the One who hears all and knows all.

## Verse 3:152

> Indeed, Allah fulfilled His promise to you when you ˹initially˺ swept them away by His Will, then your courage weakened and you disputed about the command and disobeyed, after Allah had brought victory within your reach. Some of you were after worldly gain while others desired a heavenly reward. He denied you victory over them as a test, yet He has pardoned you. And Allah is Gracious to the believers

## Verse 3:154

> Then after distress, He sent down serenity in the form of drowsiness overcoming some of you, while others were disturbed by evil thoughts about Allah—the thoughts of ˹pre-Islamic˺ ignorance. They ask, “Do we have a say in the matter?” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “All matters are destined by Allah.” They conceal in their hearts what they do not reveal to you. They say ˹to themselves˺, “If we had any say in the matter, none of us would have come to die here.” Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Even if you were to remain in your homes, those among you who were destined to be killed would have met the same fate.” Through this, Allah tests what is within you and purifies what is in your hearts.
